Advance when all three vowels cross with no flip and no shout, then deliberately retire the scaffold by opening the same lines into the whoop-hey mix of 13.8.
- Source: Feminization docx line 87, Complete Vocal Technique curbing; Sadolin Complete Vocal Technique (sibling books directory, too large to commit; cited by title).

### 13.7 Chiaroscuro whisper — tuning resonance without fold wear

- Purpose: This drill tunes the two resonances in every vowel with no fold vibration, so ear and articulators learn the bridge with zero phonatory cost. Chiaroscuro is bright-dark balance: the bright ringy over-vowel carried by F2, sensed high in the tall back room of the pharynx, plus the warm close under-vowel carried by F1, sensed out front in the fore-mouth. The whisper is not a mouthy effortful stage whisper that shoves the larynx up; it is internalized generous low-effort turbulent airflow like an inward Darth Vader breath with almost no jaw or lip movement, nearly ventriloquist. Under a sincere affect, F1 bandwidth narrows until the whisper itself carries audible pitch. Matching those pitches in soft phonation while keeping the warm under-vowel present builds bright-warm balance across the passaggio, so sung vowels later already know where to sit.
- Setup: Sit or stand with the jaw barely moving and tongue free. Work one family at a time from the Bozeman table: /a/ as in father with under-colors /ʌ/ as in cup to /ɔ/ as in thought to /ʊ/ as in book to /o/ as in go; dark /ɒ/ as in lot with /ʊ/ to /o/; /e/ as in say to /ɛ/ as in bed with passive rounding toward /œ/ as in French coeur; and the home-base /ŋ/ as in sing to /a/ to /o/ sequence. Give each minute one inward affect, never broadcast: mischief, skepticism (duh I knew that), disgust, empathy (aw cute puppies), or soothing self-reassurance. The affect narrows the bandwidth that lets whisper pitch emerge.
- Execution: Breathe the whisper with minimal fore-mouth movement, top corners of the back room high in an internal pharyngeal grin while the warm under-vowel stays present out front. Adjust the pharynx, not the jaw, until the faint pitched ring clarifies, then match it in soft phonation on the same vowel without changing the internal shape. For home base, slide 1-3-1 on /ŋ/ with tongue edges anchored to the upper molars, add a crisp double-g to seal the palate into /a/, form /o/ with the upper lip only and no pucker, inhaling beforehand on a relaxed snore image and ascending as if drinking water.
- Sensation: High ring in the back room plus warm closeness out front at once, large color changes from almost no mouth movement, whisper sounding pitched rather than white, matched sung vowel simultaneously bright and warm. Effort near zero; throat tiredness means the whisper turned mouthy.
- Pitfall plus correction: Spread mouthy whisper with jaw motion and larynx hike gives white noise with no pitch and teaches the wrong coupling; shrink all visible movement, breathe inward, reload a sincere affect, and let pitch emerge. Thin hiss with no warmth lost the under-vowel; restore front warmth before chasing ring. Jaw opening on /e/ to /ɛ/ must freeze so pharynx and affect do the migration.
- Dose plus move-on: About five minutes, one affect per minute across the families, is coaching judgment; the source gives affects, targets, and choreography but no duration. Advance when low warmth and high ring sound together and the matched sung vowel keeps both. If no whisper pitch emerges, change affect rather than pushing; skepticism and mischief most reliably narrow the band.
- Source: Feminization docx lines 51 to 54 with table lines 55 to 79; Bozeman Episode 375 https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ilAZ8JaOQMA, modeling PDF http://www.kenbozeman.com/resources/Chiaroscuro_Whisper_Vowel_Modeling.pdf, resources http://www.kenbozeman.com/Pedagogic-Resources.php, passaggi review https://voicestudycentre.com/news/after-the-session-training-the-passaggi-of-classical-genre-voices-with-ken-bozeman/.

### 13.8 Whoop, hey, and vowel migration — the turn itself

- Purpose: This drill installs the central Bozeman skill: letting the vowel turn by itself below whoop timbre, steering it once inside whoop, and blending whoop warmth with hey ring into continuous mix. Passive migration means holding tract shape stable while rising harmonics do the work: as H1 then H2 rise above F1 and lose boost, the vowel shades toward a closed cousin (/a/ toward /ʌ/ or /ɔ/, /ɛ/ toward /e/) with no mouth change. Active modification means adjusting the articulators once F0 itself nears F1 and the voice enters whoop, opening or tracking close vowels so they stay lit. Done right, the turn happens with no shout, squeeze, or dull hole.
- Setup: Start mid-voice on open vowels, moderate volume, light edge, jaw stable, lips unspread, larynx floated. Know both calls cold: whoop as You-hoo across a street while waving (H1-dominant warmth and release), hey as Hey (upper-harmonic ring). The mix keeps whoop underneath with hey on top.
- Execution: Ascend slowly on /a/ and /ɛ/ holding the shape frozen, refusing jaw widening or lip spreading, and let the passive shading arrive unassisted; this is the turn below whoop. As F0 nears F1 and sound warms toward pure whoop, modify actively: adjust articulators on close and mid-close vowels, track F1 with F0, open just enough to stay lit, and blend hey ring back so the result is neither hoot nor shout. Descend in reverse. Alternate open and close vowels across rounds.
- Sensation: Passivity feels like doing nothing while the vowel shades itself with no push at the old effort point; activity feels like small precise adjustments restoring ping without squeeze; the mix feels warm behind the palate and bright in the mask together. Its opposite, called yell (F1-H2 coupling dragged up by opening or larynx hiking with swallowing muscles, pressed and loud), is unmistakable throat work by contrast.
- Pitfall plus correction: Pure whoop too early on /i/ or /u/, or with cartoon hollowness, goes dull and squeezes compensatorily; delay whoop on close vowels, keep the dorsum high-forward, and feed hey in sooner. Jaw widening through the turn traps a heavy shout; freeze width, let the passive cousins arrive, and trust harmonic crossing over mouth opening.
- Dose plus move-on: Slow ascending-descending scales on two open and two close vowels, about four round trips with support resets between, is coaching judgment; the source states the passive-active rule and synthesis with no reps. Advance when the turn runs with no shout, squeeze, or dull patch and close vowels keep ring unforced.
- Source: Feminization docx lines 47 to 50; Bozeman 2010 https://faculty.lawrence.edu/bozemank/wp-content/uploads/sites/10/2014/03/Bozeman_JOS-2010_JanFeb-.pdf; migration paper https://www.academia.edu/38807736/VOWEL_MIGRATION_AND_MODIFICATION; Whoop It Up Part 1 https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OfihqBqITKs; BAST 172 https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BishgGhj_zg.

### 13.9 Voce faringea third-peak cultivation — turning falsetto into tenoral tone


- Purpose: Build the specific adducted M2-dominant coordination the docx calls voce faringea, and cultivate its acoustic signature — a third spectral peak around 1400–1800 Hz at near chest-voice level — so the upper bridge stops sounding like falsetto and starts reading to a listener as tenor voice. The mechanism is worth stating plainly because the name gets misused. Falsetto alone is cricothyroid-dominant vibration with thin, stretched folds and a relatively open quotient; it sounds flute-like but weak because the epilaryngeal outlet above the folds is wide, so little of the harmonic energy is reflected back to assist the folds and little high-frequency energy is radiated. Chest voice sounds strong partly because thick-fold closure generates strong upper harmonics and partly because a narrower outlet clusters resonances 3–5 into the singer's-formant region. Voce faringea keeps the thin-fold M2 vibration but adds two narrowings: lateral pharyngeal-wall approximation at the faucal pillars (the side walls beside the uvula draw in) and aryepiglottic narrowing at the epilaryngeal tube outlet (the rim above the folds narrows without recruiting the false folds). The combined narrowing raises inertive reactance — the acoustic push-back that helps the folds close cleanly each cycle — and clusters the higher resonances so the long-term average spectrum grows a third peak between roughly 1400 and 1800 Hz, measured in the cited data at about −51 dB against −52 dB for chest, effectively parity. That peak is what the ear reads as "chest-like" even though the muscle basis is head-dominant. Without it, every attempt to "strengthen" the top adds thyroarytenoid mass and drags chest upward; with it, loudness and carrying power come from resonance gain rather than fold collision. This drill isolates the narrowing first at low cost, then loads it with messa swells, then proves it survives vowel change.
- Setup: Confirm the section-12 light edge first with one messa di voce gradient or one minute of 3.0–3.5 mm straw phonation, because voce faringea built on heavy folds is just pressed chest with a narrow throat and will ache. Stand buoyant, sternum comfortably high, jaw free, tongue tip forward with lateral edges lightly touching the upper molars. Pick a pitch in the upper first bridge where falsetto is easy but thin — for most low tenors/baritones E4–G4 on /a/ or /o/. Have a straw nearby only as a reset. Work at moderate volume; this drill fails if you chase forte before the peak exists. If you use a spectrogram app, set it to long-term average spectrum so you can check the 1400–1800 Hz band after the fact, but do not stare at it while singing — the ear and the faucal sensation lead, the display only confirms.
- Execution: Phase 1 — find the walls. On the chosen pitch, alternate three times between a plain breathy falsetto /hu/ and a bratty infant "wae" cry on the same pitch, keeping pitch and loudness roughly constant. The "wae" is the finder: the cry reflexively approximates the lateral walls and narrows the aryepiglottic rim, which you hear as a sudden brassy edge and feel as side-wall nearness at the pillars. Freeze that wall posture and carry it onto a sustained /a/ for 4–6 seconds, keeping the cry's edge inside the rounder vowel. Repeat on /o/ and /u/, because each vowel places F1/F2 differently and the walls must learn to hold across all three. Phase 2 — load it. On the same pitch, sing a pinpoint messa di voce inside the wall posture: attack pianissimo with clean edge contact, swell to forte over about 4 seconds, return to pianissimo over 4 seconds, walls unmoving while only fold mass and air change. The forte peak should add brilliance in the 1400–180o Hz sense — a laser-like core — rather than width or shout. Phase 3 — move it. Sing a slow ascending-descending fifth (e.g. D4–A4–D4) on /a/ with walls held, then the same fifth on /o/, listening for the core persisting through every semitone. If any note goes hooty, do not push; re-touch it with one "wae" onset on that pitch, then re-enter the scale. End by singing one short repertoire fragment (one bar) with the walls alive inside the words, proving the coordination survives consonants.
- Sensation: Correct engagement feels like a narrow, tall column behind the palate with side-wall presence at the pillars — often described as a "pharyngeal grin" high in the back room — while the throat front stays quiet and the larynx rides level. Sound feels larger than its effort: moderate breath produces a ringing, slightly reedy core with sympathetic buzz at the bridge of the nose and hard palate, and chest-wall vibration increases modestly at the forte peak without the sternum thud of dragged chest. The pianissimo ends feel thread-like but unbroken, the same voice made smaller. Wrong engagement feels like a squeeze at the front of the throat, ache at the sides of the neck after the set, or a louder-but-duller tone — all signs the false folds joined and the outlet collapsed into whole-throat constriction rather than the two precise narrowings.
- Pitfall plus correction: The dominant failure is building the peak with pressure: louder, pressed phonation that raises the whole spectrum including noise, feels effortful, and leaves hoarseness. The correction is to halve the volume, return to the "wae" finder at pianissimo, and rebuild the swell slower — brilliance must bloom on a steady core, never arrive as a shove. The second failure is losing the walls on vowel change, typically /a/ keeping the core while /u/ goes hooty because lip rounding widened the outlet. The correction is to form /u/ and /o/ with the upper lip only, no pucker, keep tongue edges on the molars, and think the back room tall while the front stays small. The third failure is breathy falsetto that never adducts: walls approximated but folds apart, so air pours through with no peak. The correction is a firmer "wae" edge — adduction first, then walls — or one preceding bright "nay" for closure memory before re-entering the sustained vowel. Any soreness, urge to cough or clear, or loss of upper pianissimo ends the drill for the day; straw phonation only afterward.
- Dose plus move-on: The docx gives the voce faringea definition and spectrum data but no dose, so dose is coaching judgment. Use three wall-finder alternations per vowel (/a/, /o/, /u/), two pinpoint messa swells per vowel, and two slow fifth-crossings per vowel per session — roughly 10–12 minutes. Advance when the forte peak on all three vowels carries audible core with no ache and the fifth-crossing keeps the core unbroken, at which point retire the "wae" finder and enter scales on plain vowels with walls pre-set. If a spectrogram is available, advance when the 1400–1800 Hz band on the messa forte peak sits within a few dB of your own chest-voice reference on the same vowel; if no analyzer is available, advance on the ear criterion of a listener mistaking the top for chest at moderate volume with zero throat cost.
- Source: Feminization docx lines 80–82 (voce faringea definition as adducted falsetto-dominant mix with lateral wall approximation plus aryepiglottic narrowing; third-peak LTAS data −51 vs −52 dB); docx lines 84–87 (bratty finder sounds, pinpoint messa loading); Bozeman chiaroscuro PDF (back-room vs fore-mouth orientation; minimal visible articulation, tall pharynx).

### 13.10 Curbing expansion — restraint-to-release ladder out of the first bridge


- Purpose: Turn the single-paragraph curbing mention in the docx into a complete, exitable pathway. Curbing, from Complete Vocal Technique, is a temporary restrained phonation used roughly across the first bridge into the mix above: a mild complaint-like or withheld-moan hold on mid-closed vowels that meters airflow and caps fold mass so chest cannot be dragged upward. Its value is specific to the low-tenor/baritone problem: the first bridge (roughly C4–F4 depending on vowel) is where thyroarytenoid habit is strongest, so "free" singing through it usually means shouting through it, while "light" singing through it usually means flipping out of it. Curbing fences the line — neither loud nor breathy — by adding just enough metering resistance that the fold mass stays capped while cricothyroid stretch does the pitch work. But curbing is a scaffold, not a tone: left in place it becomes a pressed, covered moan that cannot open into the whoop–hey mix the bridge ultimately requires. This drill therefore has two halves with an explicit exit — four restrained connections per vowel, then deliberate release into open mix on the same line — so the singer learns both the fenced-in feeling and the moment to retire it. The restraint is small throughout: belt, whine, sob, and yawn are all wrong neighbors, and the drill names each so they can be refused.
- Setup: Work D4–F4 to start on one mid-closed vowel at a time: "uh" as in hunger first, then narrowed "oh", then softened "ee". "Mid-closed" matters because fully open /a/ invites shout and fully closed /i/ invites squeeze; the middle heights keep F1 in a range where passive migration can do part of the work. Stand upright but not rigid, support engaged with sternum high and lower ribs expanded, a faint complaint or withheld-moan expression on the face — never a smile (which spreads and brightens into shout) and never a sob (which depresses the larynx and hollows into Underfull). Set moderate volume and refuse to exceed it; curbing at forte is just belt with a moan painted on. Keep the tongue edges near the upper molars so vowel identity cannot drift while the hold does its work.
- Execution: Step 1 — establish the hold. On a single pitch (e.g. E4), sustain "uh" for 6 seconds with the mild complaint restraint on, listening for a covered, slightly held tone that is neither loud nor breathy. It should feel like singing with the handbrake lightly on. Step 2 — connect. Sing a stepwise ascending-descending fourth or fifth through the bridge (e.g. C4–F4–C4) on the same vowel with the identical hold, refusing both the urge to open into shout ascending and to release into air descending. Hold each connection for a full phrase rather than note-hopping; the continuity is the training. Do four clean connections on "uh", then four on narrowed "oh", then four on softened "ee". Step 3 — release. Immediately after the fourth clean connection on each vowel, sing the same line once more with the hold deliberately retired: same pitch path, same support, but the moan restraint released into the whoop–hey mix (whoop warmth underneath, hey ring on top, per 13.8), allowing the vowel to open one step toward its sung form. Alternate restrained and released passes (restrained, released, restrained, released) until the released pass keeps the connection the restraint built. Finish by singing a two-bar repertoire line first restrained on the vowel of its stressed syllable, then released on the words, proving transfer.
- Sensation: Correct curbing feels covered and slightly held, narrow but not squeezed, with support more involved than in free singing because it meters air against the hold — the abdominal wall feels engaged in fine gradations rather than braced. The old break point is crossed without a bump; there is no flip, no shout, and no ache afterward. The tone is deliberately restrained and a little unglamorous, and that restraint is the fence. Correct release feels like the same connected line with the handbrake off: the sound opens, warms (whoop) and rings (hey) at once, volume stays moderate while presence grows, and the throat stays as quiet as it was inside the hold. Wrong curbing feels like pushing against yourself — throat work, jaw widening, volume creeping — which means chest mass rode up under the restraint and the fence failed.
- Pitfall plus correction: Shouting through the hold is the commonest failure: volume jumps ascending, the jaw widens, the vowel spreads toward /a/, and the line arrives loud and pressed. The correction is to halve the starting volume, narrow one step toward "ee", reset the complaint expression before ascending, and refuse any mouth widening — the line must stay small to stay connected. Breathiness inside the hold is the opposite failure: restraint collapsed into abduction (folds parted), air pours through, tone goes hollow. The correction is a slightly firmer complaint edge with steadier airflow, never more push — think "metered" rather than "forced". Sob coloring (dark, hooty, larynx dropped) means the moan slid into depressor effort; the correction is to re-anchor the tongue high-forward (NEE edges on molars) and lighten the expression toward withheld speech rather than weeping. Yawn hollowness means the space enlarged to fake ease; the correction is a faint smile-narrow and a smaller mouth. If any correction needs more than two resets on one vowel, drop to straw phonation for one minute and re-enter a semitone lower.
- Dose plus move-on: Four connected holds per vowel plus one released pass per vowel per session is coaching judgment; the docx names curbing on mid-closed vowels up to the mix with no dose. Advance a vowel when its four restrained connections cross with no flip and no shout and its released pass keeps the connection with audible whoop–hey blend. Advance the drill when all three vowels ("uh", narrowed "oh", softened "ee") meet that standard in one session, at which point deliberately retire the scaffold: subsequent bridge practice opens into free mix from the start, keeping one restrained pass only as a reset for new cracking material. If the released pass always breaks, the hold is being used as a crutch — stay one more week on alternating restrained/released pairs before attempting fully open lines.
- Source: Feminization docx line 87 (curbing on mid-closed vowels to connect the first two bridges, Complete Vocal Technique lineage); docx lines 47–50 (whoop/hey synthesis the release opens into); docx lines 88–93 (weight-order logic: restraint meters mass rather than adding it).

### 13.11 M2-closure staircase — from airy Hoo to Wee to Nay slide (Stoney Ep. 89/87/88 arc)


- Purpose: Give head-mechanism closure a graded staircase instead of a single leap. Many low tenors live in one of two stuck states: pressed chest dragged too high, or a disconnected airy falsetto ("Hoo") that has pitch but no edge and therefore cannot bridge into anything. The Stoney arc across the cited episodes orders the repair as a closure gradient: begin in deliberately airy M2 phonation where the folds are thin and stretched but barely meeting, then add incremental adduction through "Wee" (lip-rounded forward closure with twang edge), then convert to a "Nay" slide that carries the now-closed thin edge through the passaggio on an equilibrium syllable. Each step is a small, reversible increase in edge contact — never a press — so the singer learns what "just enough closure" feels like at every point on the ramp. Physiologically the staircase raises the closed quotient from near-zero (airy Hoo, most of each cycle open, much airflow wasted) toward roughly half the cycle (firm Wee/Nay, clean edge meeting without thickening), while keeping cricothyroid-led stretch dominant throughout so thyroarytenoid mass never re-enters. The payoff is a head voice that can get louder without getting heavier, which is the precondition for every bridging syllable in 13.4–13.5 to work.
- Setup: Work in the comfortable head-voice zone first (roughly G3–C5 for orientation, then the actual bridge zone), on a drone or piano reference for pitch honesty since airy phonation drifts flat. Posture buoyant, jaw free, lips available for rounding (Hoo/Wee) then spreading-narrow for Nay. Start at pianissimo; the entire staircase stays quieter than feels necessary, because loudness is the cue that most reliably summons chest mass and collapses the experiment. Keep a tissue or hand before the mouth for the airflow check described below, and keep the 3.5 mm straw nearby: if closure is ever lost completely, one straw glide restores edge contact faster than any amount of throat effort.
- Execution: Stage 1 — airy Hoo baseline (Stoney Ep. 89 territory). On a comfortable mid-head pitch, sigh a breathy "Hoo" (as in hoot) for 4–6 seconds, deliberately airy: thin, hooty, much airflow on the hand. Sustain three such sighs, then glide one slow octave slide (e.g. C4–C5–C4) on the same airy Hoo, feeling cricothyroid stretch with minimal fold meeting. This is calibration, not target tone — it maps the full stretch range with zero press. Stage 2 — Wee firming (Ep. 87 territory). Without changing pitch zone, switch to "Wee" (as in week) with lips rounded-forward and a faint twang edge, sung at the same quiet volume. The /w/ onset with its lip rounding plus the high-front /i/ vowel reflexively firms edge contact: airflow on the hand should drop noticeably while ring appears. Sustain three 6-second Wee tones, then glide the same octave slide on Wee, keeping the lip rounding and forward placement through the top. If the top flips to breath or cracks, narrow toward a brattier Wee (more twang edge, never more push) and halve the speed. Stage 3 — Nay slide (Ep. 88 territory). Convert to bratty "Nay" (rhymes with day) with /n/ mask direction plus bright /eɪ/: sing the octave slide and then a 1-3-5-8-5-3-1 arpeggio from comfortable mix to just above the secondo passaggio (e.g. C4–C5), keeping the vowel relentlessly bright and forward over the top and back down. The /n/ aims energy into the palatal mask while the twangy vowel keeps the folds meeting through the thinning trajectory. Alternate Wee and Nay across rounds (Wee slide, Nay slide, Wee arpeggio, Nay arpeggio) to contrast lip-rounded closure with mask-directed equilibrium. Finish with one slow stepwise line on pure vowels (/i/ then /u/) using the closure memory with no consonant help, proving the edge survives without training wheels.
- Sensation: The staircase has a legible airflow signature on the hand: Hoo moves much cool air with little sound; Wee moves clearly less air with more ring; Nay moves least air with a continuous buzzing thread through the mask. Internally, Hoo feels wide and hooty with vibration diffuse; Wee feels narrower with a tickle behind the upper teeth and the outlet firming at the pillars; Nay feels like a laser thread pulled bottom-to-top with no gap, the top narrow and pingy rather than big. Support involvement rises slightly across the stages (firmer closure needs steadier air) while throat effort stays near zero throughout — any neck-side bulge or ache means press replaced closure. Pitch should feel centered at every stage; flatness belongs to the airy end and should resolve by the Nay stage as closure firms.
- Pitfall plus correction: Collapsing back to breath at the top of any slide means adduction was lost, usually by rounding or softening the attitude ("being pretty") or by running short of air. The correction is to re-brat (bite toward "nyeh"), halve the speed, restart a third lower with the top pre-imagined small and forward, and ensure support was set for the full slide before phonating. Shouting or jamming at the top means chest was dragged up under the closure — the singer added mass instead of edge. The correction is to restart quieter on Wee (never louder), think the top smaller, and confirm the larynx rode level with a finger lightly on the thyroid cartilage. Pressed, clicking onsets (folds slammed before airflow) mean the quest for closure overshot into glottal attack; the correction is a silent-/h/ thought before each start so air leads by a fraction of a second. If Wee firms but Nay breaks, the /n/-to-vowel handoff is leaking — sing "N-N-Nay" with prolonged /n/ closure crisply made, then shorten the /n/ across repetitions until the vowel inherits its closure.
- Dose plus move-on: Three airy Hoo sighs plus one Hoo slide, three Wee sustains plus two Wee slides, and three Nay slides plus three Nay arpeggios per session is coaching judgment; the episodes supply the Hoo→Wee→Nay order and the slide/arpeggio shapes, not counts. Advance a stage when its slides keep unbroken ring with the expected airflow signature (Hoo airy but on-pitch, Wee less air with ring, Nay least air with mask thread). Advance the drill when the closing pure-vowel line threads with no consonant help. Keep the staircase as a permanent warm-up in miniature (one slide per stage) whenever the voice arrives breathy or pressed; it is the fastest honest test of which end of the closure gradient needs work that day.
- Source: Stoney Episodes 89 (airy-Hoo M2 orientation) https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-rfwlXnRbzY, 87 (Wee closure firming) https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KaMJ9YvnUNw, 88 (Nay slide conversion) https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=obzz4a3tO4U, full episode pages https://www.voicelessonstotheworld.com/episodes/ep-89-how-to-sing-falsetto-flageolet-vocal-register-world-tour-part-4 and https://voicelessonstotheworld.com/episodes/ep-88-how-to-sing-mix-voice-vocal-register-world-tour-3 and https://voicelessonstotheworld.com/episodes/ep-87-how-to-sing-head-voice-vocal-register-world-tour-2; feminization docx line 87 (nay equilibrium syllables; octave slides and arpeggios as the bridging shapes); docx lines 103–112 (light-edge precondition: straw/hum and messa-gradient closure memory the staircase assumes).

### 13.12 Twang ignition — wae, nyeah, neeyeow to 70% conversational ring


- Purpose: Install aryepiglottic twang as a voluntary, dosable control rather than a character effect. Twang here means narrowing of the aryepiglottic sphincter (the rim above the folds) with a shortened, megaphone-like pharynx, high-front /i/ posture, elevated velum sealing the nose, and no false-fold compression — the oral-twang posture of the docx. Its acoustic leverage is large: the cited measurements move output from roughly 67 dB neutral to 74 dB low twang to 88 dB high twang on comparable effort, because the narrowed outlet raises inertive reactance (folds close more efficiently) and clusters higher resonances for carrying power. That leverage is exactly why twang is the ignition for bridging: a small, precise narrowing buys the ring that lets loudness stop meaning weight. But full character twang (baby cry, taunt, race car) is far too much for repertoire — left at 100% it sounds brassy, edgy, and effortful, and it tempts the singer to push against the narrowing. The drill therefore over-shoots deliberately to 100% to find the mechanism, then dials back about 70% into a conversational ring that keeps the efficiency while sounding natural. The thumb-under-chin monitor and the pinched-nostril check below make the dose honest.
- Setup: Start on a comfortable mid pitch (speaking range or low bridge, e.g. C4–E4) where character sounds are easy. Stand with the tongue pre-set high-front (/i/ dorsum high-forward, edges near molars, tip near lower teeth) and the velum lifted (the gentle height of the start of a yawn, without dropping the jaw). Place a thumb lightly under the chin on the floor of the mouth as a pressure sensor — any downward bulge during twang means the tongue root is pushing and the mechanism is wrong. Work at first in private: the finder sounds are deliberately unglamorous, and self-consciousness tightens the very constrictors this drill must leave out. Playfulness is a technical requirement, not a mood suggestion.
- Execution: Round 1 — baby cry "wae" (as in wail). Imitate an infant wail on a repeated pitch or short siren, bratty and nasal-edged, until a sharp brassy edge appears for little air. Round 2 — playground taunt "nyeah" (as in nyah-nyah). Repeat the taunt across a fifth, keeping /n/ closure crisp and the vowel biting. Round 3 — race-car "neeyeow" with its glide, tracing a small siren up-down. Each finder should produce the unmistakable twang signature: sudden loudness and presence jump with no extra push, buzz high behind the palate, thumb quiet under the chin. Then the dial-back: on the same pitch, speak-sing a plain sentence ("how are you today") starting at 100% character twang and reducing in three steps to roughly 30% retained — the docx's "dialed back about 70%" — until the voice sounds like a vivid conversational tone with ring inside it rather than a character. Alternate finder and dial-back (wae → sentence, nyeah → sentence, neeyeow → sentence), then sing one bridge scale on "nyeah" dialed to conversational, proving the dosed twang survives pitch change. Check nasality once per session by speaking the sentence while pinching the nostrils: muffle or buzz against the fingers means the velum dropped and twang is leaking into hypernasality; insert one crisp double-g between an /ng/ hum and /a/ or /i/ to retrain clean palate lift until pinched and unpinched sound identical.
- Sensation: Full twang feels like a megaphone switched on: small effort, large presence, ring concentrated high behind the nose-bridge with a faint tickle at the upper teeth, throat uninvolved. The dial-back feels like keeping that megaphone's core while removing its caricature — the voice stays forward and carrying at conversational volume, with the thumb still quiet and the velum still high. Correct dosing has a volume signature: the sentence at 30% retained twang should be clearly more present than its neutral version at identical effort, without any sense of shouting. Wrong twang feels like pushing into a narrowing tube — neck sides firm, chin floor bulging into the thumb, sound louder but duller — which means false-fold compression joined and the outlet collapsed into squeeze.
- Pitfall plus correction: Bracing (tongue-root push into the thumb, jaw clench, neck ache) is the primary failure; it converts inertive gain into constrictor strain. The correction is to stop, return to straw or soft /m/ hum for one minute, re-approach at half volume with a smaller mouth and a brattier attitude — twang is found by attitude and posture, never by force. Hypernasality (velum dropped against raised dorsum, energy leaking into the nose) is the second failure, heard as a honky or muffled color and felt as buzz against pinched nostrils. The correction is the double-g drill (/ng/ hum → crisp /g/ /g/ → /a/ or /i/) until the palate lift is clean, then re-enter the sentence. Leaving the dial at 100% (singing everything brassy) is the third failure; it reads as edgy and fatigues across a session. The correction is the three-step reduction ritual on every new phrase until 30%-retained becomes the default, reserving full twang for ignition only. Any ache, hoarseness, or urge to clear ends twang work for the day.
- Dose plus move-on: Three finder-to-sentence cycles (wae, nyeah, neeyeow) plus one dialed bridge scale per session is coaching judgment; the docx supplies the three finders, the ~70% dial-back, the dB ladder, and the double-g/nostril checks, not counts. Advance when each finder ignites ring within two attempts with a quiet thumb, the sentence at dial-back keeps conversational ring with pinched/unpinched identity, and the bridge scale holds the dosed twang unbroken. At that point twang becomes a pre-set (one finder onset before any bridge line) rather than a drill, and repertoire phrases are sung at the retained dose with the finder available only as a reset.
- Source: Feminization docx lines 123–127 (NEE/twang/velopharyngeal triad; wae–nyeah–neeyeow finders; ~70% dial-back; 67→74→88 dB ladder; thumb monitor; nostril-pinch and double-g seal checks); Bozeman chiaroscuro PDF (tall back-room pharynx vs spread fore-mouth; minimal visible articulation).

### 13.13 Whistle-siren past B5–C6 on minimal air — the top extension without press


- Purpose: Extend usable range past the second bridge into the whistle-adjacent top (B5–C6 zone) while proving that altitude needs less air, not more. The failure it cures is universal in low tenors approaching the extreme top: the singer treats height as effort, shoves air, hikes the larynx with swallowing muscles, and either jams into pressed chest or evacuates into breath. Both are pressure responses to a tuning problem. Above roughly the second bridge, the first formant can no longer be tracked by mouth opening without dragging the larynx up (the "yell" coupling the docx warns against), so the voice must transition toward flageolet/whistle-adjacent mechanics: very thin, short vibrating fold segments with a narrow, stable outlet and exceptionally low airflow. This drill approaches that zone only by siren — continuous glides with no consonant attacks, no repertoire pressure, no volume target — on the smallest viable airstream, so the nervous system learns that the top is a place of reduced flow and increased focus rather than increased force. It is range extension by efficiency, and it doubles as a diagnostic: any note that demands more air is a note whose tuning is not yet found.
- Setup: Warm the staircase first: one airy-Hoo slide, one Wee slide, one Nay slide (13.11), plus one dialed-twang sentence (13.12), so closure and outlet are already honest before altitude is attempted. Work on lip-rounded forward vowels (/u/ as in blue, then /o/) because their long narrow outlets stabilize the top; open /a/ at altitude invites yell-opening and is banned in this drill until /u/ threads. Set pianissimo as a hard ceiling — if the siren cannot be done quietly it cannot be done correctly — and keep support buoyant with ribs expanded but not held out. No piano App needed; the glide is continuous and the ear judges smoothness, not hit pitches. Stop conditions are absolute: any ache, tickle-to-cough, hoarseness, or loss of pianissimo ends the drill for the day with straw cool-down only.
- Execution: Step 1 — enter on /u/. Starting around C5, siren slowly up a fifth and back (C5–G5–C5) on a lip-rounded /u/ with upper-lip-only rounding (no pucker), tongue edges on the molars, back room tall. Move at roughly half the speed that feels natural; every semitone must keep thread-like connection. Step 2 — extend. Across sessions, extend the siren apex by semitone only while clean: C5–G5, then C5–A5, toward B5 and C6 over weeks, never forcing a new apex in one day. The descent retraces the ascent; do not release into breath coming down — the thread must survive both directions. Step 3 — thin the air. On each apex, run a minimal-air check: repeat the siren with deliberately less airflow than feels necessary, listening for the tone persisting on what feels like almost nothing. The correct top uses astonishingly little air; if reducing air kills the tone, closure lapsed (add Wee-edge memory) rather than airflow being insufficient. Step 4 — transfer vowels. Once /u/ threads to the current apex, repeat on /o/ formed with the upper lip only, then on /i/ with dorsum high-forward and smile-narrow (the hardest, attempted last). Do two sirens per vowel per session maximum — this is potent, fatiguing work despite its quietness. Finish with one slow descending fifth on /u/ from the apex back into the bridge, proving the top reconnects downward without a break.
- Sensation: Correct top sirens feel tiny, focused, and almost self-sustaining: a thin laser thread high behind the palate with minimal breath movement, support springy but idle, throat disinterested, the apex notes feeling narrower and more "above" than "pushed". Airflow on a hand before the mouth is barely perceptible — the signature of whistle-adjacent efficiency. The descent feels like reeling the thread back in rather than falling. Wrong sirens feel like climbing: breath expenditure rising with pitch, neck sides firming, larynx hiking, tone spreading or jamming, the apex either shouted or evacuated. Any sense of altitude-as-effort is the signal to stop ascending and consolidate the current apex.
- Pitfall plus correction: Pushing air for height is the cardinal failure — louder, breathier, duller sirens that arrive flat and tired. The correction is to halve the air, halve the volume, halve the speed, and re-attempt; height is found by smaller, never by bigger. Yell-opening (widening the jaw and spreading the lips to track F1 upward past the point where F1 can usefully track the fundamental, hiking the larynx with swallowing muscles) is the second failure, heard as a brassy shout that cracks or chokes near the apex. The correction is to freeze jaw width, return to /u/, think the back room taller while the front stays small, and let the whistle-adjacent mechanics take over rather than chasing resonance by opening. Larynx-hike soreness means swallowing constrictors joined; the correction is immediate stop, one minute of straw phonation, and re-entry a third lower with the inward, soothing affect of the chiaroscuro work (mischief or self-reassurance) rather than a reaching affect. Breath-evacuation at the apex (tone winking out) means closure thinned past the edge; the correction is one Wee onset on the apex pitch for closure memory, then re-siren at once.
- Dose plus move-on: Two sirens per vowel (/u/, /o/, then /i/), maximum six sirens per session, is coaching judgment; the B5–C6 destination and minimal-air constraint are stated in the setup above, not counted from any source. Extend the apex by semitone only when the current apex threads twice consecutively on /u/ with minimal air and silent throat, and hold each new apex for three consecutive clean sessions before extending again. Advance vowels from /u/ to /o/ to /i/ only while the prior vowel holds. Treat this drill as a two-to-three-times-weekly extension stimulus, never daily to fatigue; on off days, one gentle /u/ siren to the established apex maintains without loading. Any session with apex ache, next-day huskiness, or lost pianissimo means the apex was forced — retreat a semitone and rebuild.
- Source: Whistle-siren drill spec as stated in the setup above; feminization docx lines 47–50; docx lines 80–87; Bozeman passaggi review and Whoop It Up Part 1. Dose is coaching judgment per section 18.

## 14. Articulation and prosody drills




Articulation is where the tongue, lips, jaw, and soft palate shape the sound after the vocal folds have made it. Prosody is the melody, rhythm, and stress pattern laid across whole sentences. Listeners judge gender less from any single vowel than from this running texture: where consonants sit, how vowels hang together, whether emphasis arrives as a pitch lift or a loud push, and whether laughs and sighs match the speaking voice or fall back into an older heavier habit. A low tenor or baritone brings a strong low mechanism and a lifetime of masculine habits, namely forceful bursts, short clipped vowels, a narrow pitch band, and stress made by getting louder. The work here replaces those habits without adding squeeze at the throat.

Do this section only on thinned folds. Weight means how much fold mass participates in each cycle, heard as buzz and felt as chest thud. Resonance size means how small or large the throat and mouth spaces are, heard as brightness or hollowness. Heavy folds plus a small throat produce the trapped buzzy strained quality called overfull, and no careful consonant work fixes that state. So confirm the light smooth setting from section 12 first, usually with one messa di voce gradient (a slow swell from soft to loud and back on one vowel) or one straw breath, and keep everything in section 14 quiet and forward. Quiet does not mean weak or breathy. It means the same clean closure made with less collision force, so the tone stays clear while the articulators do the brightening. If buzz returns at any point, stop the sentence work and re-thin before continuing, because drilling articulation on heavy folds rehearses the wrong coordination.

### 14.1 Heat from fire calibration chain

- Purpose: This one phrase rehearses every articulatory and glottal target in a single sentence, so it serves as both diagnostic and daily reset. It starts airflow before the folds close, which prevents the hard slammed start that drags weight in. It then parks the tongue high and forward, which raises the resonant peaks of the vocal tract, called formants, toward their brightest ceiling. Higher formants are heard as brightness and felt as vibration high behind the upper teeth rather than thud in the chest. The rest of the phrase tests whether that bright frame survives a lip consonant, an American r, a neutral vowel, and an open diphthong. Where the frame collapses names the repair.
- Setup: Stand or sit in the balanced support posture, sternum comfortably lifted, lower ribs expanded sideways, jaw hanging free. Find the light flute-like phonation from section 12 first and keep that effort. Choose a comfortable mid speaking pitch, not a sung high pitch. Keep a straw nearby only as a reset tool.
- Execution: Say "heat from fire, fire from heat" in three stages: syllable by syllable with pauses, then three times at a slow walking pace, then three times at natural conversational pace without letting the tongue fall back. Keep conversational volume and the upper part of the comfortable speaking range throughout. The /h/ (the h sound as in heat) is a voiceless breath made at the open glottis, meaning the folds stay apart while air flows, and it must sound like a soft sigh turning into voice rather than a scrape. The /i/ (the ee vowel as in see) is made with the tongue dorsum, meaning the broad upper surface of the tongue, arched high and forward toward the hard palate while the tip rests near the lower teeth, leaving little empty mouth space. The coda of heat in connected speech surfaces as /ʔ/ (the glottal catch as in the middle of uh-oh, here standing in for an unreleased t), a brief check of the airstream at the folds while the tongue blade, the flat part just behind the tip, stays high in the ee posture instead of dropping. The /f/ (the f sound as in fire) is made with the upper incisors, the front top teeth, lightly touching the lower lip, which narrows the mouth exit and pulls attention to the extreme front. The /ɹ/ (the American r sound as in red) is made with tip and blade raised toward the ridge behind the upper teeth and lips kept unrounded and relaxed, not pursed. The /ə/ (the schwa, the uh sound as in the a of about) in from is the short neutral vowel, and the /aɪ/ (the igh sound as in fire) glides from open toward ee. Both must stay inside the same high forward frame as the ee, without the root pulling back into a dark hollow.
- Sensation: When it is right, the phrase feels as if it happens behind the upper teeth. The h feels like warm air starting before tone, the ee like a thin bright beam with a faint tingle at the upper incisors, the checked ending like a gentle valve tap rather than a throat grab, the f like a light tooth-on-lip tickle, and the r narrow and forward rather than swallowed. Vibration sits high in the mask, the cheek and nose-bridge region where sympathetic buzz is felt, and the chest stays quiet.
- Pitfall plus correction: The common failure is a hard glottal attack on heat, meaning the folds slammed shut before air moved, heard as a click and felt as a jab in the throat, which drags mass into the whole sentence. Return to a silent-h image, breathing the h a full beat before tone joins, and redo the phrase on one straw breath so airflow leads. A second failure is a dark r, heard as a swallowed cave-like honk with the tongue root bulging back and lips rounding. Raise the tip toward the upper ridge, spread the corners into a faint relaxed smile, and speak the r slowly until the front tingle returns. A third failure is dropping from and fire into a throaty yawn space, heard as sudden dull bigness. Freeze the dorsum high after heat and speak the rest without letting the jaw fall open.
- Dose plus move-on: Three slow syllable passes plus three slow linked passes plus three conversational passes, once daily as the articulation reset. Move on when all nine keep the forward tingle and light closure with no jab and no darkening. If a pass fails, take one straw breath and one messa gradient, then attempt once more rather than grinding repetitions.
- Source: Feminization docx lines 130 to 160, phoneme table and mechanics for the heat from fire phrase from TransVoiceLessons practice.

### 14.2 Forward sibilant and stop tuning

- Purpose: Sibilants (long hisses like s and sh) and stops (brief taps like t and d) set the perceived placement of whole sentences. Their brightness is fixed by the small cavity in front of the tongue constriction: a smaller front cavity rings higher, heard as sharper and more forward, while a larger or lip-rounded cavity rings lower, heard as duller. Moving contact slightly forward on the upper ridge with unrounded lips therefore lifts the consonant spectrum with no extra blowing. This matters because a dull hiss makes even a light vowel sound heavier by contrast, while a bright hiss holds the ear forward through the sentence.
- Setup: Keep the light fold setting and high forward tongue from 14.1. Work before a mirror so the lips are visible. Rest tip and blade lightly near the upper alveolar ridge, the bumpy gum ridge just behind the upper front teeth. Hold the lip corners subtly retracted in a relaxed smile, never protruded or rounded into a tube. Keep airflow modest, because blasting air to force brightness adds fold pressure and defeats the drill.
- Execution: Speak "she sees three green trees" three times at moderate pace, listening only to the hisses. The /s/ (the s sound as in see) and the /ʃ/ (the sh sound as in she) are the long hisses, and the /t/ (the t sound as in tea) and /d/ (the d sound as in do) are the brief taps on the same ridge. Place each contact forward with smiled lips, make each hiss just long enough to hear its color, then release cleanly into the vowel with no gap or gulp. Then whisper s-sh-s-sh three times on one breath to hear the noise color without fold vibration confusing the ear, then speak the sentence again with voice.
- Sensation: Correct hisses feel like a fine cool spray at the back of the upper teeth with faint energy at the lip corners and almost nothing in the throat. Taps feel like light touches on the ridge rather than hammer blows. The following vowels then arrive already forward with no repositioning. Wrong placement feels thick and low, with the vowel starting dull and needing rescue.
- Pitfall plus correction: Pulling the tip back and rounding the lips makes a mushy low hiss and drags the sentence backward. Check the mirror for smile corners, slide the tip forward until the hiss pitch audibly rises, and shorten the hiss so anxiety does not push extra air. Overblowing to fake brightness is the second failure, heard as harsh spray with throat tightening and quick breath loss. Halve the air and trust the smaller front cavity to do the brightening, rechecking that the folds are still light. Never fix a dull s by pressing harder.
- Dose plus move-on: Three spoken repetitions plus one whispered s-sh check plus three spoken repetitions, once daily after 14.1. Move on when the final three keep bright hisses and immediate forward vowels with no extra air and no rounding. If dullness persists, reset with one slow ee hold rather than drilling louder.
- Source: Feminization docx line 162, consonantal spectral envelope and lip embouchure passage on forward placement and smiled corners.

### 14.3 Vowel chaining on a high-front anchor

- Purpose: Masculine habit lets back vowels fall into a large dark throat space that re-masculinizes the sound even when pitch and weight are correct. Chaining trains the opposite: every vowel is a modification of the same high forward ee posture, so the back of the tongue stays elevated and the jaw refuses the cavernous corner. Formant peaks stay high across the chart, heard as consistent brightness rather than a bright ee alternating with a swallowed oh.
- Setup: Stay on the light setting at a comfortable mid speaking pitch. Anchor with a sustained ee, feeling the lateral edges, the sides of the tongue, lightly touching the upper molars while the dorsum stays high and the tip rests near the lower teeth. Keep the jaw barely open, roughly a finger-width, and make vowel changes mostly with lips and mid-tongue rather than big jaw swings.
- Execution: Speak or intone "mee, mae, mah, moh, moo" on one pitch, holding each syllable about two beats. The vowels are /i/ (ee as in see, already defined), then /e/ (the ay sound as in may), then /æ/ (the a sound as in cat, the open front step), then /ɑ/ (the ah sound as in father, the darkest legitimate step), then /o/ (the oh sound as in go), then /u/ (the oo sound as in blue). Move stepwise without resetting to neutral between steps, and refuse the extra-dark /ɒ/ (the rounded o sound as in British lot, the jaw-dropped throat vowel of the dark lower corner). Nearing that sound means the jaw has fallen and the dorsum has collapsed. Repeat the chain three times, first slowly with pauses to adjust, then linked as one gliding phrase. Keep the posterior dorsum, the back of the tongue surface, elevated throughout, felt as fullness high under the soft palate rather than pressure low in the throat.
- Sensation: When it is right, all steps feel like one family in the same front room. The ee is brightest and narrowest, later vowels get rounder but never leave the forward wall, and even ah and oh keep a thread of ee tingle behind the teeth. The throat feels uninvolved and the jaw lazy. When it is wrong, back vowels feel as if they drop down a shaft, and buzz slides from the mask into the throat.
- Pitfall plus correction: Opening the jaw wide on mah and moh for false clarity drops the resonance peaks into hollowness and flattens the tongue. Freeze the jaw after mee, shape later vowels with lip rounding and small tongue glides, and accept a slightly narrower mah that matches the chain. Letting the dorsum fall while the lips fake the vowel makes a muffled disconnected moo. Re-anchor the tongue edges to the molars on a fresh ee, then re-enter the chain without losing that edge contact.
- Dose plus move-on: Three full chains daily, first slow then linked, on one comfortable pitch. Move on when all steps sound like one continuous placement with visibly little jaw travel in the mirror. Then transpose up two semitones and confirm again, since surviving pitch change is the real test.
- Source: Feminization docx line 162, vowel brightening and chaining passage after Sandy Hirsch and Seattle Voice Lab protocols.

### 14.4 Legato softening of consonant attacks

- Purpose: Masculine articulation is often muscular, meaning forceful stop bursts with clipped short vowels and audible gaps before each vowel. Each hard burst briefly re-thickens the folds, because the glottis braces against the pressure buildup, and each clipped vowel cuts resonance short. This drill reverses both: consonant-to-vowel transitions soften and vowel durations extend slightly, producing legato, the smooth connected flow in which more of each sentence is resonant vowel. Legato is acoustic, meaning more resonance per sentence, and protective, meaning the folds stop getting slammed by stops.
- Setup: Use one short declarative sentence at the light setting, for example "may we meet at noon." Take only a modest breath, since a huge breath invites muscling through. Keep the jaw free and the tip forward so softening does not decay into mumbling.
- Execution: Speak the sentence three times with exaggerated linking. Melt each consonant into its vowel with no silent gap and minimum puff, especially the /m/ (the m sound as in may) and /n/ (the n sound as in noon) and /w/ (the w sound as in we), which should start already voiced and forward rather than attacked. Shorten every stop burst to the minimum that keeps words intelligible, and lengthen each vowel roughly a third beyond the clipped habit, so the sentence rides one breath ribbon rather than stepping word by word. Then speak it once at natural speed keeping the same melt. If a word blurs, restore just enough edge for intelligibility, never the full burst.
- Sensation: Correct legato rides one unbroken airstream with the folds buzzing steadily underneath and the tongue dancing lightly on top, with no belly jerking and no throat jab on initials. The vowels feel long and exposed, which can feel vulnerable to a singer used to hiding inside consonants, but that exposure carries the feminized resonance.
- Pitfall plus correction: Confusing soft with breathy or mumbled loses word edges as the folds go airy and unfocused. Softening means less burst pressure, not less closure. Keep the light clean closure from 14.1, sharpen the contact point while reducing its force, and shorten rather than blur the consonant. Stretching vowels by drawling the whole sentence is the second failure; it drops pitch and weight toward baseline. Keep tempo normal and steal the extra vowel time from the consonant gaps instead.
- Dose plus move-on: Three exaggerated linked repetitions plus one natural-speed repetition on one sentence daily, rotating sentences across days. Move on when the natural-speed version sounds smooth with every word intelligible and the throat jab-free. Then apply the same linking to one real conversation sentence as the transfer test.
- Source: Feminization docx line 162, passage contrasting forceful bursts and clipped vowels with softened transitions and extended vowels for legato flow.

### 14.5 Prosody transfer with pitch excursions

- Purpose: Prosody is the lowest-hanging fruit here because listeners infer gender from melody before analyzing vowels. Masculine habit marks stress by getting louder with heavier closure, called amplitude stress with higher closed quotient, meaning the folds spend more of each cycle shut. That burst re-thickens the voice for a moment and undoes the light setting. This drill marks stress by gliding upward in pitch while holding weight light and steady, so emphasis arrives as melody rather than force. It also widens total sentence range, replaces falling droops into fry with rising or arching turns, and adds tiny pauses for a buoyant cadence. Fry means pulse-register creak, the low popping of slack folds, avoided at phrase ends because it instantly re-masculinizes the close.
- Setup: Keep the light setting and forward placement, since prosody on heavy folds only makes a louder heavy voice. Pick three short sentences with natural emotion, for example "what a great day" and "I love warm-ups," plus one real sentence from daily life. Engage the support posture so lifts come from balanced airflow and gentle cricothyroid tilt, the lengthening action that raises pitch without squeezing, rather than from shoving air or gripping the throat.
- Execution: Speak each sentence three times. Mark the stressed word by stepping it up in pitch while holding loudness flat: the word glides upward, often by a third to a fifth, while weight stays at the light edge. Widen the whole sentence to roughly twice the habitual monotone band, letting unstressed syllables float lower without falling into creak. At each clause boundary, each natural break between thought chunks, end with an upward or circumflex inflection, a rise or rise-fall arch, instead of the falling droop. Insert one or two micro-pauses, beat-long breath-silent gaps, in longer sentences so rhythm lilts rather than marches. Suspect every loudness peak: a word that got louder gets redone with pitch alone. Finish with one unscripted real sentence under the same rules, since drilled sentences alone do not transfer.
- Sensation: Correct prosody feels like dancing on a steady light ribbon that rises to meet stressed words, with the throat uninvolved and the lift coming from breath buoyancy and slight lengthening tilt. Clause ends feel open and airborne, micro-pauses like buoyant catches rather than stops. Chest thud on stressed words means loudness has replaced pitch.
- Pitfall plus correction: Spiking volume while believing it was pitch is the standard failure, heard as a bark with a throat grab. Place a hand on the sternum, redo the word at half volume with an exaggerated glide, and accept a smaller interval that stays light over a bigger one that barks. A perpetual question lilt on every phrase is the second failure; it sounds affected and cranks the larynx by swallowing muscles. Vary the boundary turns between rises and rise-fall arches, confirming ring without pressing. End-of-sentence fry is the third failure, a gravelly rattle on the last word. Keep air moving through the final vowel and lift the boundary turn slightly until the rattle clears.
- Dose plus move-on: Three repetitions each on three sentences plus one real unscripted sentence daily. Move on when stresses lift with no loudness spike, clause ends arch rather than fry, and the real sentence keeps lightness throughout. Record one sentence weekly, since the ear forgives in the moment what playback reveals.
- Source: Feminization docx lines 163 to 164, prosody passage on pitch excursions replacing volume spikes, wider range, boundary inflections, and micro-pauses.

### 14.6 Non-verbal resets and sentence integration

- Purpose: Laughs, sighs, and interjections are reflexive, firing from old motor memory without planning, so they revert to the heavy baseline even when drilled speech stays light. One chesty laugh can undo five careful sentences perceptually. Connected paragraphs then test whether the phoneme frame, sibilant placement, vowel anchor, legato, and melody survive together at conversational speed with attention divided, which is where old habits ambush the speaker. Attempt this only once 14.1 through 14.5 are each stable.
- Setup: Stay at the light setting with articulators pre-placed: a silent /h/ or /i/ (both defined in 14.1) with dorsum high, lip corners in the faint smile, and the velum, the soft palate flap sealing the nose, gently lifted as at the start of a yawn but without dropping the jaw. This head-dominant mix, phonation led by the lengthened mechanism rather than chest muscle, is the launch pad for every non-verbal. Choose a comfortable mid to upper speaking pitch, never the low guffaw pitch, plus one real paragraph of about four sentences from a book, message, or lyric.
- Execution: Produce three gentle sighs as "hee" from the h-ee posture, descending slightly while keeping the forward tingle and lifted palate. Then three small laughs as "hee-hee-hee" on one breath, each pulse from the same h-ee valve rather than a throat grab, light and forward with no bark. Then three interjections such as "oh," "wow," and "no way," each launched pre-set with a rising or arching turn rather than a falling grunt. Keep all versions small and believable, not falsetto giggles. Then read the paragraph three times: first slowly, pausing after each sentence to name any dull consonant, dark vowel, loudness spike, or fry ending and fixing it with its parent drill; second at natural pace with no stops to train continuity; third with mild load added such as walking, gesturing, or mirror eye contact, since divided attention is the transfer agent. Finish with one sentence from memory looking away from the page.
- Sensation: Correct non-verbals start above the folds with air gathering into tone rather than a throat clutch, with pulses high behind the palate, the sigh a warm forward slide, the palate high and wide, and no urge to cough or clear afterward, which signals the false folds, the cushions above the true folds, stayed out. A passing paragraph feels like sustained forward ease across minutes with attention on meaning while tongue and lips handle placement underneath. Fatigue, if any, sits lightly in tongue and lips, never as throat ache.
- Pitfall plus correction: Letting amusement drop the setup produces a first laugh pulse an octave low with a bark. Shrink the laugh by half, raise its start a step, and re-initiate from an audible h so air demonstrably leads. Jamming the palate to force seal produces pinched pressure behind the nose; elevated means gently lifted, not jammed. Release to an open ah with no nasal buzz at the nostrils, then re-lift lightly until pinched and unpinched nostril checks sound identical. Drilling the paragraph louder to project reintroduces amplitude stress everywhere; cut volume by a third and restore melody and forwardness, since brightness carries intelligibility better than force. Any throat ache or hoarseness ends the session per section 19.
- Dose plus move-on: Three sighs plus three laughs plus three interjections daily during training, plus one paragraph three times three days per week. Move on when non-verbals launch pre-set without thought and two consecutive paragraph sessions keep forward placement, legato, pitch-led emphasis, and fry-free endings with no throat cost. Record the third pass monthly: travel should show narrower jaw travel, brighter hisses, longer vowels, and wider but natural melody.
- Source: Feminization docx lines 163 to 164, paralinguistic passage on h-ee initiation in head-dominant mix with elevated velum; paragraph integration follows lines 130 to 164, with dose as coaching judgment per section 18.

## 15. Long holds and agility as readouts




There is no vibrato drill in these books, deliberately. Vibrato here is a readout, an involuntary shimmer appearing only when support, adduction, and resonance already balance, never something manufactured by shaking the jaw, pulsing the belly, or rolling the larynx. The healthy window is 5.0 to 7.0 cycles per second with half to one semitone extent, synchronized across harmonics and tallest on upper partials. Below about 4.5 Hz wide reads as slack adduction or weak anchoring; above about 7.5 Hz narrow reads as squeeze or locked support; flat lines blooming late read as stylistic delayed onset rather than fault. Holds therefore read support steadiness, because only a quiet larynx with paced air and stable resonance lets vibrato emerge alone. Agility reads only after weight is stable, because fast lines expose faked lightness through tongue-root pushing and jaw shake. Running agility on heavy folds rehearses the pushing it should detect, so it waits. All four drills are diagnostic first, athletic second: the sound reports the system, and the prescription targets support, weight, or placement rather than the wobbling note.

### 15.1 Ten-second holds — the support-steadiness readout

- Purpose: Ten seconds is long enough that support leak, adduction drift, or resonance collapse must appear as pitch drift, level change, wobble, or absent vibrato. Plain vowels on six pitches give six samples, and the holds preset adds measured drift, stability, and vibrato-presence fields checking the ear against data. Late-blooming vibrato on one combination, straight tone first with shimmer arriving once the system settles, is the desired readout: proof the larynx stayed quiet while airflow paced itself. A six-second messa at G3 and C4 adds swell-and-fade samples at bridge pitches.
- Setup: Stand with sternum high, lower ribs expanded, articulators relaxed, weight thinned to a light edge. Rotate plain vowels (/a/, /i/, /u/) across six pitches from low-mid to upper-mid at holdable moderate volume with no shaping planned. Sing each from breath first, then with the preset measuring, so felt sense calibrates before numbers appear.
- Execution: Sustain each vowel ten full seconds with no intentional vibrato, dynamics, or vowel morphing, tract frozen and air paced from support. Then run the preset on the same pitches for drift, stability, and vibrato presence, plus a six-second messa swell-and-fade at G3 and C4. Map which combinations bloomed late, stayed straight, or drifted; that map is the prescription.
- Sensation: A steady hold feels uneventful: tone hangs in the mask with minimal throat awareness, air feels small and even, any vibrato arrives uninvited as gentle upper-partial shimmer rather than visible shake. Drift feels like pitch wanting to sag or sharpen; instability like narrowing, spreading, or beating. Both are information, not failure.
- Pitfall plus correction: Pulsing the belly, shaking the jaw, or rolling the larynx makes tremolo, a manufactured wrong-rate wobble, invalidating the readout and rehearsing tension. Hold steady, accept straight tone, and record it straight. Chasing preset numbers with pushed volume or brightness also invalidates; keep straw ease from section 8, where quieter-steady outranks louder-unsteady.
- Dose plus move-on: Six ten-second holds plus one preset run and two six-second messa shapes is the session dose and, as session design, coaching judgment. Advance when one combination blooms effortlessly, then use it as the reference sensation. With none blooming, prescribe support and weight work, not more holds.
- Source: Readout fields per SINGING_DIAGNOSTICS lines 19 to 21, 40, 48; section 7 marker table for the 5.0 to 7.0 Hz window and delayed-onset reading. Dose is coaching judgment per section 18.

### 15.2 Drone against alone — separating ear from production

- Purpose: One hold can fail for two unrelated reasons: the singer cannot hear center without help, or cannot produce steadily even with help. Holds with a drone (a continuous reference on the working pitch) versus without separate them. Large drift alone with steadiness on-drone means production works and the pitch image fades, an audiation task. Wavering even with the drone means production itself is unsteady, a support, adduction, or resonance task. Practice then targets the right system instead of drilling breath for an ear fault or ear for a breath fault.
- Setup: Reuse the 15.1 pitches and vowels with a drone source (app drone, keyboard, or generator) just audible beneath the voice, never masking. Alternate strictly per pitch, drone then alone, so the alone attempt follows fresh memory without cross-pitch carryover.
- Execution: Sustain with the drone, listening for beating slowing to zero and the felt lock of fusion, then immediately repeat the same vowel and pitch without it, holding the same image and support. Log per pitch whether drift appears alone-only, with-drone, both, or neither, keeping volume, vowel, and effort identical so the drone is the only variable.
- Sensation: Lock feels like settling into a groove with brightening resonance as harmonics align; retention feels like the groove persisting from memory with unchanged support. Ear-only failure feels like gradual sliding inside a beautiful tone; production failure like narrowing, spreading, or trembling under a clear reference.
- Pitfall plus correction: Confusing them misassigns weeks of work: breath drills for a steady-with-drone voice needing only pitch memory, or ear drills for a wavering-with-drone voice needing support. Trust the pairs literally: steady-with-drone plus drifting-alone means audiation work with pitch-anchor and hum recall; wavering-with-drone means production work in sections 9, 10, and 12. Singing louder over the drone to cover it also voids the test; keep the drone faint.
- Dose plus move-on: One drone-then-alone pair per pitch across the six is coaching judgment as session design. Advance when alone matches drone within preset stability tolerance on most pitches. If one pitch alone separates, shed to that combination until it transfers.
- Source: Readout per SINGING_DIAGNOSTICS lines 19 to 21; section 7 markers for drift-versus-production reading. Dose is coaching judgment per section 18.

### 15.3 Pentatonic agility — fast lines that expose pushing

- Purpose: A five-note run at speed exposes work that slow singing hides, because there is no time to re-grip with jaw or tongue between notes. It reads whether the section 12 light edge and section 13 equilibrium survive tempo. The ladder is half-speed staccato (short detached notes with clean releases, never jabbed), then half-speed legato (unbroken connection, no scooping), then full speed sung deliberately lighter. Honest agility is a clean line with still jaw, forward tongue tip, and quiet floor-of-mouth, proving extrinsic muscles stayed out.
- Setup: Confirm stable weight with a clean light messa gradient from 12.2 first; agility on heavy folds rehearses pushing at speed. Take a comfortable five-note pentatonic cell low in the easy range, transposing up by semitone only while clean. Face a mirror with two fingertips resting lightly under the chin as sensors on the floor-of-mouth; any pushing presses into them.
- Execution: Sing the cell half-speed staccato with precise onsets and releases, then half-speed legato with centered connected steps, then full speed lighter than feels necessary. Keep tongue tip forward, jaw still, fingers quiet through all three speeds. Do three rounds per speed per transposition, dropping back a semitone at the first smudge, scoop, or finger pressure.
- Sensation: Clean agility feels small, forward, almost self-articulating from steady air with a disinterested throat; support feels springy, pulsing lightly per staccato note and floating level in legato. Warnings are unmistakable: visible jaw shake, root bulge into the fingers, or facial reaching for high notes, all meaning edge precision yielded to extrinsic effort.
- Pitfall plus correction: Jaw shake or tongue push means the line is muscled, usually from attempting full speed before half-speed legato was clean. Slow down, restore the edge with one straw breath or soft hum, and rebuild staccato to legato before touching full speed. Scooping means closure lapsed at speed; sharpen half-speed staccato with silent /h/ imagery per note. Louder-to-go-faster means support is pushing; sing smaller and more forward instead.
- Dose plus move-on: Three rounds per speed per transposition is coaching judgment; the staccato-to-legato-to-lighter ladder follows the R&B protocol in `docs/spectrogram-references.md`. Advance transposition only while full speed stays clean with quiet fingers and still jaw. Above a boundary pitch that always collapses, hold the boundary until three consecutive sessions stay clean.
- Source: `docs/spectrogram-references.md` R&B Styles section (High-Velocity Melisma, Laryngeal Decoupling, Micro-Agility); sections 9 and 10 mechanics for staccato-legato support.

### 15.4 Vowel isolation — connection without consonant help

- Purpose: Consonants like /n/, /g/, /m/, /b/ are training wheels adding mask direction, posterior closure, or mini-occlusion through the passaggio. This drill removes them, running the line on pure vowels, usually /i/ then /u/ across the bridge with no tempo pressure, so any remaining break comes from vowel-formant handling itself. Close front /i/ (dorsum high-forward, minimal oral volume) gives maximum formant frequencies and the brightest ceiling; close back /u/ (long narrow rounded outlet) tests rounding without darkening. Threading bare vowels proves text will thread.
- Setup: Take a slow stepwise line crossing the bridge on /i/ first then /u/, barely above conversational volume, slow enough to hear and adjust every semitone. Keep section 13 choreography ready: frozen shape for passive migration ascending, small active adjustments at whoop warmth, hey ring blended not forced. No mirror, sensors, or speed goal; this is listening.
- Execution: Sing consonant-free from the vowel itself with clean simultaneous air-and-closure onset, slowly enough to notice migration and allow passive shading toward closed cousins below whoop without intervening. Where a pitch threatens break, narrow one step toward /i/ rather than pushing or widening, and loop that two-note link slowly until it threads. Do two slow passes per vowel at straw ease before alternating.
- Sensation: One continuous placement with vowel identity intact: /i/ stays /i/ without spreading toward /e/, /u/ stays rounded without hooting; the bridge feels narrow and tall rather than wide; support feels nearly idle because the tract tunes. A clean pass brings the quiet satisfaction of no rescue.
- Pitfall plus correction: Break on a bare vowel almost always means too open a vowel for the pitch, parking F1 where H2 collides, previously masked by consonants; narrow toward /i/ and repeat slower, never louder. Breathiness means onset lost adduction unaided; borrow one preceding nay for closure memory, then return to the bare vowel at once. Yawn-darkening means the larynx dropped for warmth; smile-narrow slightly and re-anchor the tongue high.
- Dose plus move-on: Two slow passes per vowel is coaching judgment; the consonant-free cross-bridge pattern follows `docs/spectrogram-references.md`. Advance when /i/ and /u/ both cross clean slowly, then carry the line into text vowels and repertoire. If text re-breaks, narrow text vowels for repetitions rather than adding speed.
- Source: `docs/spectrogram-references.md` R&B Styles section; Bozeman migration framing https://www.academia.edu/38807736/VOWEL_MIGRATION_AND_MODIFICATION and Whoop It Up Part 1 https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OfihqBqITKs.

### 15.5 DSP calibration protocol — 3-second neutral /a/ plus full-range siren


- Purpose: Give the app's DSP pipeline a known-good reference every session before any graded drill runs, so later numbers (drift, stability, vibrato presence, spectral tilt) read the voice rather than the room, the microphone distance, or the warm-up state. The problem this solves is silent but large: phone microphones apply their own gain riding, noise suppression, and high-pass filtering; rooms add early reflections that smear stability readings; and an uncalibrated singer brings an unknown mixture of breathiness, press, and placement into the first graded hold, so the preset cannot tell poor support from a cold start or a bad mic position. The protocol has two halves that calibrate two different things. The 3-second neutral /a/ at comfortable mid pitch fixes input gain and spectral-tilt baseline: /a/ as in father is the most open, least tract-filtered vowel, so its long-term spectrum most honestly reports the source balance between fundamental and upper harmonics (tilt toward 0 = pressed/heavy, toward 1 = breathy, near ~0.35 = balanced speaking target per the diagnostics lineage). The full-range siren then maps the microphone and the voice across the actual working range — bottom to top on one breath-efficient glide — exposing dropouts, octave-jump artifacts, room nodes, and the pitches where tracking fails before they corrupt a graded hold or agility run. Together they take under two minutes and convert every later readout from a possibly-spurious number into a compared-against-baseline judgment. Run it before 15.1–15.4 and before any preset-graded bridge line, every session, no exceptions.
- Setup: Quiet room, same mic position every session (hand's-breadth distance, slightly off-axis to avoid breath blasts; if using a headset, identical boom placement). Same comfortable mid pitch for the /a/ each session (e.g. A3 for most low tenors — low enough to be unstrained, high enough to carry harmonics). Same moderate volume: the /a/ should be a natural speaking-effort "ah", never a sung forte, because gain set on forte clips the later pianissimo work and gain set on whisper over-amplifies the later forte work. Open the app's level meter (or any input meter) alongside the preset under test. Have water available; dry-mouth raises noise-floor friction that mimics breathiness on tilt.
- Execution: Step 1 — silence floor. With the meter running, hold 3 seconds of room silence to read the noise floor; note it. Any HVAC, fan, or traffic rumble visible here will live inside every later stability number, so kill what can be killed. Step 2 — neutral /a/. On the reference pitch, sing a steady 3-second /a/ at speaking effort with the section-12 light edge, tract neutral (no twang, no sob, tongue resting, jaw barely open). Sustain without vibrato, dynamics, or morphing. Read gain (peak level), tilt/balance, and stability on that window: adjust input gain so the /a/ peaks in the preset's mid-target zone with headroom above, then freeze gain — do not touch it again for the session. If tilt reads pressed (toward 0) or breathy (toward 1) against the ~0.35 balanced zone, do not "fix" it with mic moves; fix it with one straw breath or one messa gradient and re-sing the /a/ until the voice itself is balanced — the calibration must report the voice honestly, not be gamed. Step 3 — full-range siren. On lip-rounded /u (most tracking-stable vowel), glide slowly from the bottom of the comfortable range to the top of the current working range and back (e.g. G2–G5–G2 or current apex) at moderate volume with unbroken thread, watching the tracker's pitch trace throughout. Mark any pitch where the trace drops out, octave-jumps, or lags; those pitches are excluded from graded judgments today (sing them ungraded) or re-sirened slower to confirm. Step 4 — lock.
